### Question 140: Male Genital Pathology: Focus 03-40 (• Clinical findings can include fever (acute prost))
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, clinical presentation, and histological features of acute prostatitis, specifically differentiating it from other causes of male pelvic pain and fever, and identifying the characteristic finding on microscopic examination. (本题考察前列腺炎的病理生理学、临床表现和组织学特征，特别是区分急性前列腺炎与其他引起男性盆腔疼痛和发热的原因，并识别显微镜检查中的特征性发现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe lower back pain, perineal pain, dysuria, urinary frequency, and urgency for the past 3 days. He reports chills and fever (102.5°F / 39.2°C) that started yesterday. He denies recent sexual activity or trauma. His medical history is significant for benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH), managed with tamsulosin. Physical examination reveals a tender, boggy prostate gland on digital rectal examination (DRE). Urinalysis shows pyuria (15 WBCs/hpf) and bacteriuria. Urine culture is pending. The patient is started on intravenous antibiotics. A transrectal ultrasound (TRUS) is performed, which shows a diffusely enlarged and hypoechoic prostate gland. A subsequent prostate biopsy is performed 6 weeks later, after resolution of the acute symptoms, to rule out malignancy, given his age and BPH history. Microscopic examination of the biopsy specimen reveals numerous neutrophils infiltrating the prostatic stroma and acini, along with occasional giant cells. No evidence of malignancy is found.

Which of the following histological findings is most characteristic of the patient's initial condition?

(A) Granulomatous inflammation with caseating necrosis within the prostate gland.
(B) Lymphocytic infiltration with germinal centers and ductal obliteration.
(C) Neutrophilic infiltrate within the prostatic stroma and acini, often accompanied by abscess formation.
(D) Squamous metaplasia of the prostatic epithelium with keratin pearls.
(E) Atypical glandular cells with increased nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio and loss of polarity.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male. Age is relevant for BPH and prostate cancer risk.
- **Presenting Symptoms:** Acute onset (3 days) of severe lower back pain, perineal pain, dysuria, frequency, urgency, chills, and high fever (102.5°F). These symptoms strongly suggest an acute inflammatory process involving the genitourinary tract, specifically the prostate. The constellation of urinary symptoms plus systemic signs (fever, chills) points towards prostatitis. Lower back pain can be referred pain from the prostate.
- **Pertinent Negatives:** Denies recent sexual activity (less likely STI-related prostatitis) or trauma.
- **Medical History:** BPH managed with tamsulosin. BPH can predispose to urinary stasis and infection, increasing the risk of prostatitis.
- **Physical Exam:** Tender, boggy prostate on DRE. This is a classic finding in acute bacterial prostatitis, indicating inflammation and swelling of the gland.
- **Urinalysis:** Pyuria (15 WBCs/hpf) and bacteriuria. These findings confirm a urinary tract infection, likely involving the prostate.
- **Imaging:** TRUS shows diffusely enlarged and hypoechoic prostate. This is consistent with inflammation and edema in acute prostatitis.
- **Biopsy Timing:** Performed 6 weeks *after* acute symptoms resolved. This is crucial. The biopsy is *not* from the acute phase but is performed later to rule out malignancy.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Numerous neutrophils infiltrating the prostatic stroma and acini, occasional giant cells. No malignancy. This describes the histological features of *acute* prostatitis, which is the condition the patient initially presented with, even though the biopsy was done later. The question asks for the characteristic finding of the *initial condition*.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most characteristic* histological finding of the *patient's initial condition* (acute prostatitis).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Acute Bacterial Prostatitis:** Typically caused by Gram-negative bacteria (e.g., *E. coli*) ascending from the urethra or refluxing into the prostatic ducts.
- **Pathophysiology:** Bacterial infection triggers an acute inflammatory response within the prostate gland. Neutrophils are the primary inflammatory cells recruited to the site of infection. They infiltrate the prostatic stroma (connective tissue) and the acini (glandular structures).
- **Histology:** The hallmark of acute prostatitis is a dense neutrophilic infiltrate within the prostatic stroma and acini. Abscess formation (collections of neutrophils and necrotic debris) can also occur. Giant cells (multinucleated cells formed by the fusion of macrophages) may be present but are less specific.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's symptoms (fever, chills, dysuria, frequency, perineal/back pain), physical exam findings (tender, boggy prostate), and urinalysis results (pyuria, bacteriuria) are all consistent with acute bacterial prostatitis. The biopsy findings, although obtained later, confirm the histological features expected in this condition.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Granulomatous inflammation with caseating necrosis is characteristic of tuberculosis (TB) prostatitis. While TB can affect the prostate, it typically presents more insidiously, often with chronic symptoms, and the histology is distinct (granulomas, caseation). The patient's acute presentation with high fever makes TB less likely.
- (B): Lymphocytic infiltration with germinal centers and ductal obliteration is characteristic of chronic lymphocytic prostatitis (also known as autoimmune prostatitis or Riedel's thyroiditis-like prostatitis). This condition typically presents with chronic pelvic pain, infertility, and often lacks acute systemic symptoms like high fever. The histology is dominated by lymphocytes, not neutrophils.
- (D): Squamous metaplasia of the prostatic epithelium with keratin pearls is characteristic of squamous cell carcinoma of the prostate, a rare malignancy. This finding is not associated with acute inflammation. The biopsy explicitly ruled out malignancy.
- (E): Atypical glandular cells with increased nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio and loss of polarity are features of prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia (PIN) or prostate adenocarcinoma. These are malignant changes, which were specifically excluded by the biopsy report ("No evidence of malignancy is found").

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Acute Bacterial Prostatitis (Correct Answer) | Granulomatous Prostatitis (TB) | Chronic Lymphocytic Prostatitis | Squamous Cell Carcinoma | Prostate Adenocarcinoma |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------ | :----------------------------- | :----------------------------- | :----------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Onset**               | Acute                                       | Insidious/Chronic              | Chronic                        | Variable                 | Variable                 |
| **Symptoms**            | Fever, chills, dysuria, pain                 | Chronic pain, low-grade fever  | Chronic pain, infertility      | Variable                 | Variable                 |
| **DRE**                 | Tender, boggy                               | May be firm/nodular            | Usually normal                  | Firm/hard mass           | Firm/hard mass           |
| **Urinalysis**          | Pyuria, bacteriuria                         | Pyuria (may be sterile)        | May have pyuria                | Usually normal           | Usually normal           |
| **Histology**           | Neutrophilic infiltrate, abscesses          | Granulomas, caseation          | Lymphocytic infiltrate, germinal centers | Squamous cells, keratin pearls | Atypical glands         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute bacterial prostatitis is characterized by acute onset of fever, urinary symptoms, and pelvic pain, with a tender, boggy prostate on DRE. The key histological finding is a dense neutrophilic infiltrate. Differentiate it from chronic prostatitis (lymphocytic infiltrate), granulomatous prostatitis (TB), and malignancy (atypical cells).

### Question 141: Male Genital Pathology: Focus 03-41 (perineal, or suprapubic),painful prostate on recta)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology, clinical presentation, and diagnostic features of acute bacterial prostatitis, differentiating it from other causes of urinary symptoms and lower abdominal pain. (本题考察急性细菌性前列腺炎的病理生理学、临床表现和诊断特征，并将其与其他引起泌尿系统症状和下腹部疼痛的原因区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to the emergency department complaining of severe lower back pain, perineal pain, and urinary frequency and urgency that started abruptly 2 days ago. He reports chills and fever (102.5°F / 39.2°C). He denies dysuria or hematuria. His medical history is significant for benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH) treated with tamsulosin. Physical examination reveals a tender, boggy prostate gland on digital rectal examination (DRE). Urinalysis shows pyuria (15 WBCs/hpf) and bacteriuria. Urine culture is pending. A CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis shows a diffusely enlarged prostate gland with surrounding soft tissue edema, but no abscess formation. Given the clinical presentation and initial findings, what is the most likely underlying pathological process?

(A) Prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia (PIN) with associated inflammation.
(B) Acute bacterial prostatitis complicated by prostatic abscess formation.
(C) Chronic bacterial prostatitis with superimposed acute inflammation.
(D) Acute bacterial prostatitis with associated suppurative inflammation.
(E) Prostatic adenocarcinoma with perineural invasion causing pain.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with BPH (risk factor for urinary tract infections and prostatitis).
*   **Symptoms:** Acute onset (2 days) of severe lower back pain, perineal pain, urinary frequency/urgency, chills, and high fever (102.5°F). These are classic symptoms of acute prostatitis. The absence of dysuria is less typical but doesn't exclude it.
*   **Physical Exam:** Tender, boggy prostate on DRE. This finding is highly suggestive of acute inflammation and edema of the prostate gland.
*   **Urinalysis:** Pyuria (WBCs) and bacteriuria indicate a urinary tract infection, likely involving the prostate.
*   **Imaging:** CT scan shows enlarged prostate with surrounding edema, but *no abscess*. This rules out a complicated abscess formation.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely underlying pathological process.

The combination of acute onset, systemic symptoms (fever, chills), localized symptoms (perineal/back pain, urinary symptoms), tender/boggy prostate on DRE, and evidence of UTI (pyuria, bacteriuria) strongly points towards acute bacterial prostatitis. The CT scan specifically excludes abscess formation.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Acute Bacterial Prostatitis:** This is an acute infection of the prostate gland, typically caused by bacteria ascending from the urethra or refluxing into the prostatic ducts. Common pathogens include *E. coli* and other Gram-negative rods.
*   **Pathophysiology:** Bacteria invade the prostatic stroma, triggering an intense acute inflammatory response. This involves infiltration of neutrophils (suppurative inflammation), edema, and potentially microabscess formation (though not seen on CT here). The inflammation causes swelling and tenderness of the prostate, leading to pain (perineal, suprapubic, lower back), urinary symptoms (frequency, urgency, sometimes dysuria), and systemic symptoms (fever, chills).
*   **Histopathology:** Would show marked infiltration of neutrophils within the prostatic stroma and ducts, edema, and potentially small abscesses.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Characterized by acute onset, fever, chills, malaise, severe perineal/suprapubic/lower back pain, and urinary symptoms. DRE typically reveals a warm, tender, boggy prostate.
*   **Diagnosis:** Based on clinical presentation, DRE findings, urinalysis (pyuria, bacteriuria), and urine culture (identifying the causative organism). Imaging (like CT or ultrasound) may be used to rule out complications like abscess.
*   **Treatment:** Prompt antibiotic therapy targeting common uropathogens is crucial. Supportive care includes analgesics and hydration.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia (PIN)** is a pre-malignant lesion characterized by atypical glandular cells within the prostate. It typically does not cause acute symptoms like fever, chills, or severe pain, nor does it present with a tender, boggy prostate on DRE. While inflammation can be associated with PIN, it's not the primary pathological process causing this acute presentation.
- (B): **Acute bacterial prostatitis complicated by prostatic abscess formation.** The vignette explicitly states that the CT scan showed *no abscess formation*. While abscess is a potential complication of acute bacterial prostatitis, it is ruled out by the imaging findings in this specific case.
- (C): **Chronic bacterial prostatitis with superimposed acute inflammation.** Chronic bacterial prostatitis is characterized by recurrent UTIs and chronic pelvic pain, often without systemic symptoms like high fever and chills. While an acute exacerbation can occur, the presentation described (abrupt onset, high fever, chills, severe pain) is more typical of *de novo* acute bacterial prostatitis rather than a flare-up of chronic disease, especially without prior history of chronic symptoms. The DRE finding of a boggy prostate is also more characteristic of acute inflammation.
- (E): **Prostatic adenocarcinoma with perineural invasion causing pain.** Prostatic cancer is typically asymptomatic in early stages. While advanced cancer can cause bone pain (metastases) or perineal pain (local invasion), it does not typically present with acute onset fever, chills, urinary frequency/urgency, and a tender, boggy prostate. Perineural invasion is a feature of aggressive cancer but doesn't explain the acute inflammatory signs and symptoms.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                 | Acute Bacterial Prostatitis (Correct) | PIN (A)                 | Prostatic Abscess (B) | Chronic Bacterial Prostatitis (C) | Prostatic Adenocarcinoma (E) |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------------- | :------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------- |
| **Onset**               | Acute                               | Chronic/Insidious       | Acute (complication) | Chronic/Recurrent                 | Insidious                    |
| **Systemic Symptoms**   | Fever, Chills (Common)               | Absent                  | Fever, Chills (High) | Mild/Absent                       | Absent (unless advanced)     |
| **Pain**                | Severe, Perineal/Back/Suprapubic     | Absent                  | Severe               | Chronic Pelvic Pain               | Bone pain (metastasis)       |
| **Urinary Symptoms**    | Frequency, Urgency, +/- Dysuria      | Absent                  | Variable             | Frequency, Urgency, +/- Dysuria    | Variable (late stage)        |
| **DRE**                 | Tender, Boggy, Warm                  | Normal/Firm Nodules     | Tender, May feel fluctuance | Normal/Slightly Tender           | Firm, Nodular                |
| **Urinalysis**          | Pyuria, Bacteriuria                  | Normal                  | Pyuria, Bacteriuria  | Pyuria, Bacteriuria               | Normal                      |
| **Imaging (CT/US)**     | Enlarged, Edema                     | Normal                  | Fluid Collection     | Normal/Mild Enlargement           | Variable (late stage)        |
| **Pathology**           | Neutrophilic Infiltrate (Suppurative) | Atypical Epithelium     | Pus, Necrosis        | Neutrophils, Lymphocytes, Fibrosis | Malignant Glands             |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute bacterial prostatitis presents with acute onset fever, chills, severe localized pain, and urinary symptoms, along with a tender, boggy prostate on DRE and evidence of UTI. It is crucial to differentiate it from complications like abscess (ruled out by imaging) and chronic prostatitis (lacks acute systemic symptoms). PIN and prostate cancer do not typically present this way. (急性细菌性前列腺炎表现为急性发热、寒战、严重局部疼痛和泌尿系统症状，伴有触诊时触痛、湿软的前列腺和尿路感染证据。 必须将其与并发症（如脓肿，通过影像学排除）和慢性前列腺炎（缺乏急性全身症状）区分开来。 PIN和前列腺癌通常不会这样表现。)

### Question 142: Male Genital Pathology: Focus 03-42 ((with possible hematuria))
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis, histological features, and clinical presentation of testicular germ cell tumors (TGCTs), specifically seminoma, and its association with paraneoplastic syndromes, including gynecomastia due to hCG production. It also assesses the differential diagnosis for testicular masses and the significance of specific tumor markers. (睾丸生殖细胞肿瘤（包括精瘤）的病理发生、组织学特征、临床表现，以及与副肿瘤综合征（如因hCG产生导致的男性乳房发育）的关系。同时考察睾丸肿块的鉴别诊断和特定肿瘤标志物的重要性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of a painless, firm mass in his right testicle, which he noticed approximately 3 months ago. He denies any trauma or recent illness. He reports experiencing some breast tenderness and enlargement over the past month. Physical examination reveals a palpable, non-tender, solid mass approximately 4 cm in diameter in the right testicle. The left testicle is normal. Serum alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) is 2 ng/mL (normal < 10 ng/mL), serum beta-human chorionic gonadotropin (β-hCG) is 1500 mIU/mL (normal < 5 mIU/mL), and serum lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) is 250 U/L (normal < 250 U/L). A radical inguinal orchiectomy is performed. Histopathological examination of the resected specimen shows uniform, large, round cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and a lymphocytic infiltrate. The tumor cells are positive for PLAP (placental alkaline phosphatase) and OCT3/4, and negative for AFP.

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Yolk sac tumor
(B) Embryonal carcinoma
(C) Choriocarcinoma
(D) Seminoma
(E) Sertoli cell tumor

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male (peak incidence age for TGCTs is 15-35 years).
*   **Presenting Complaint:** Painless, firm testicular mass (classic presentation for TGCTs). Duration of 3 months suggests a relatively slow-growing process.
*   **Associated Symptom:** Breast tenderness and enlargement (gynecomastia) in the past month. This suggests hormonal influence, specifically elevated estrogen levels, often secondary to increased hCG production.
*   **Physical Exam:** Palpable, non-tender, solid mass in the right testicle (confirms the presence of a testicular tumor).
*   **Tumor Markers:**
    *   AFP: 2 ng/mL (Normal). AFP is typically elevated in yolk sac tumors and embryonal carcinomas, but not usually in pure seminomas.
    *   β-hCG: 1500 mIU/mL (Significantly elevated). hCG is produced by syncytiotrophoblast cells, which can be found in choriocarcinoma, embryonal carcinoma, and some seminomas (especially those with syncytiotrophoblastic giant cells). Elevated hCG explains the gynecomastia.
    *   LDH: 250 U/L (Borderline elevated/Normal). LDH is a non-specific marker of cell turnover and tumor burden, often elevated in TGCTs.
*   **Histopathology:**
    *   Uniform, large, round cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli.
    *   Lymphocytic infiltrate (characteristic immune response seen in seminomas).
    *   Positive for PLAP and OCT3/4 (markers strongly associated with seminoma).
    *   Negative for AFP (consistent with pure seminoma).

These findings collectively point towards a diagnosis of seminoma. The painless mass, elevated β-hCG causing gynecomastia, normal AFP, and characteristic histology (uniform cells, clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, lymphocytic infiltrate, PLAP+, OCT3/4+, AFP-) are all hallmarks of seminoma.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Seminoma Pathogenesis:** Seminomas arise from primordial germ cells. They are characterized by uniform, large cells with clear cytoplasm (due to glycogen content), prominent nucleoli, and a delicate fibrovascular stroma infiltrated by lymphocytes (often T cells).
*   **Molecular Markers:** Seminomas express specific transcription factors characteristic of germ cells, including OCT3/4 (POU5F1) and PLAP (alkaline phosphatase, placental). They typically do not express AFP mRNA or protein, even if AFP is elevated in the serum (which can occur if there's a mixed component like embryonal carcinoma).
*   **hCG Production:** Some seminomas contain syncytiotrophoblastic giant cells, which are responsible for producing β-hCG. The level of hCG elevation can vary but is often significant enough to cause gynecomastia or other endocrine effects.
*   **Clinical Significance:** Seminomas are the most common type of TGCT (~50%). They typically present as painless testicular masses. They are highly sensitive to radiation therapy and chemotherapy, making them relatively curable even in advanced stages. The presence of elevated AFP or embryonal carcinoma histology indicates a non-seminomatous component, which requires different management (primarily chemotherapy).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Yolk sac tumor:** This is the most common testicular tumor in infants but can occur in adults, often as part of a mixed germ cell tumor. Histologically, it shows Schiller-Duval bodies (glomerulus-like structures). It characteristically produces AFP, which is normal in this case. Therefore, this is incorrect.
- (B) **Embryonal carcinoma:** This is a malignant germ cell tumor composed of primitive epithelial cells. Histologically, it shows pleomorphic cells forming glands, papillae, or solid sheets. It often produces AFP and/or β-hCG. While β-hCG is elevated here, the histology described (uniform, clear cells, lymphocytic infiltrate) is not typical for embryonal carcinoma. AFP is also normal. Therefore, this is incorrect.
- (C) **Choriocarcinoma:** This is a highly malignant tumor composed purely of cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast cells. It typically presents with early hematogenous spread (lungs, brain, liver) and often causes very high levels of β-hCG. Histologically, it shows biphasic proliferation of cytotrophoblast and syncytiotrophoblast cells, often with hemorrhage and necrosis. AFP is typically negative. While β-hCG is elevated, the histology described (uniform, clear cells, lymphocytic infiltrate) is inconsistent with choriocarcinoma. Therefore, this is incorrect.
- (E) **Sertoli cell tumor:** This is a sex cord-stromal tumor arising from Sertoli cells. It can occur at any age and may produce estrogen, leading to gynecomastia or feminization in males. Histologically, it shows cells arranged in cords or tubules, often with eosinophilic cytoplasm and nuclear atypia. It does not produce AFP or β-hCG. The histology described (uniform, clear cells, lymphocytic infiltrate, PLAP+, OCT3/4+) is completely different from Sertoli cell tumor. Therefore, this is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Seminoma                                  | Yolk Sac Tumor                      | Embryonal Carcinoma                  | Choriocarcinoma                     | Sertoli Cell Tumor                 |
| :------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- |
| **Age**             | 15-35 years (peak)                        | Infants (most common), Adults (mixed) | 15-35 years                         | 20-40 years                         | Any age                            |
| **Presentation**    | Painless testicular mass                  | Painless testicular mass            | Painless testicular mass             | Often metastatic, high β-hCG        | Testicular mass, endocrine effects |
| **Histology**       | Uniform cells, clear cytoplasm, lymphocytes | Schiller-Duval bodies               | Pleomorphic cells, glands/papillae   | Biphasic (cyto/syncytio), hemorrhage | Cords/tubules, eosinophilic cells |
| **AFP**             | Negative                                  | Positive                            | Often Positive                      | Negative                            | Negative                           |
| **β-hCG**           | Often Positive (variable)                 | Negative                            | Often Positive                      | Very High Positive                 | Negative                           |
| **PLAP/OCT3/4**      | Positive                                  | Negative                            | Negative                             | Negative                            | Negative                           |
| **Gynecomastia**    | Common (due to β-hCG)                     | Rare                                | Possible (due to β-hCG)              | Common (due to very high β-hCG)     | Common (due to estrogen)          |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The classic presentation of a painless testicular mass in a young adult, associated with gynecomastia (due to elevated β-hCG), normal AFP, and characteristic histology (uniform cells, clear cytoplasm, lymphocytic infiltrate, PLAP+, OCT3/4+) is diagnostic of seminoma. Differentiating between seminoma and non-seminomatous germ cell tumors (NSGCTs) like embryonal carcinoma, yolk sac tumor, and choriocarcinoma is crucial because management strategies differ significantly (radiation vs. chemotherapy). Remember that AFP elevation points towards yolk sac tumor or embryonal carcinoma components, while very high β-hCG and biphasic histology suggest choriocarcinoma. Sertoli cell tumors are stromal tumors and do not produce germ cell markers.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Male Genital Pathology: Focus 03-43 (KP00710.indb 242)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis, histological features, and molecular mechanisms underlying testicular germ cell tumors (TGCTs), specifically seminoma, and its distinction from other testicular neoplasms. (考察睾丸生殖细胞肿瘤（包括精细胞瘤）的发病机制、组织学特征和分子机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of a painless, firm mass in his right testicle, which he noticed approximately 3 months ago. He denies any history of trauma, fever, or urinary symptoms. Physical examination reveals a palpable, non-tender, solid mass occupying the upper pole of the right testicle. The left testicle is normal. Serum tumor markers, including alpha-fetoprotein (AFP), beta-human chorionic gonadotropin (β-hCG), and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), are obtained. AFP is within normal limits (0-10 ng/mL), β-hCG is slightly elevated at 5 mIU/mL (normal < 2.5 mIU/mL), and LDH is elevated at 250 U/L (normal < 250 U/L). A radical inguinal orchiectomy is performed. Gross examination reveals a homogeneous, tan-white, lobulated mass replacing the testicular parenchyma. Microscopic examination shows sheets of uniform, large cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and a lymphocytic infiltrate. The tumor cells are positive for PLAP (Placental Alkaline Phosphatase) and OCT3/4, and negative for AFP and β-hCG. Based on these findings, what is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Yolk sac tumor
(B) Embryonal carcinoma
(C) Choriocarcinoma
(D) Seminoma
(E) Sertoli cell tumor

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, typical age range for testicular germ cell tumors (GCTs), which peak in the 15-35 age group.
- **Presenting Symptom:** Painless, firm testicular mass. This is the classic presentation for most testicular cancers, including GCTs. Painlessness is a key feature.
- **Duration:** 3 months. Suggests a relatively slow-growing process, consistent with seminoma, although other GCTs can also present this way.
- **Physical Exam:** Palpable, non-tender, solid mass in the upper pole. Confirms the presence of a testicular tumor.
- **Tumor Markers:**
    - AFP: Normal. This is characteristic of pure seminoma. Yolk sac tumors, embryonal carcinomas, and mixed GCTs often produce AFP.
    - β-hCG: Slightly elevated. While often associated with choriocarcinoma, β-hCG can also be elevated in about 10-15% of seminomas, usually at lower levels than in choriocarcinoma.
    - LDH: Elevated. LDH is a non-specific marker of cell turnover and tumor burden, often elevated in GCTs, including seminoma.
- **Gross Pathology:** Homogeneous, tan-white, lobulated mass. This appearance is typical for seminoma. Other GCTs (e.g., embryonal carcinoma) are often more hemorrhagic or necrotic.
- **Microscopic Pathology:** Sheets of uniform, large cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and a lymphocytic infiltrate. This is the classic histological description of seminoma. The clear cytoplasm is due to glycogen, and the lymphocytic infiltrate is characteristic.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Positive for PLAP and OCT3/4, negative for AFP and β-hCG. PLAP and OCT3/4 are markers commonly expressed by seminoma cells. The negativity for AFP and β-hCG further supports the diagnosis of seminoma and helps rule out other GCT components.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Testicular Germ Cell Tumors (TGCTs):** Arise from primordial germ cells within the seminiferous tubules. They are broadly classified into seminomas and non-seminomatous germ cell tumors (NSGCTs).
- **Seminoma:** Represents about 50% of TGCTs. It is characterized by uniform, large cells with clear cytoplasm (glycogen-rich), prominent nucleoli, and a characteristic lymphocytic infiltrate. Histologically, it can be classic (typical features) or variants (spermatocytic, epithelioid, etc.).
- **Pathogenesis:** Seminomas are thought to arise from germ cells that have undergone neoplastic transformation but retain some degree of differentiation, often expressing markers associated with early germ cell development (like OCT3/4, PLAP).
- **Molecular Markers:**
    - **PLAP (Placental Alkaline Phosphatase):** Ectoenzyme expressed in seminomas and other germ cell tumors, as well as in normal placenta.
    - **OCT3/4 (Octamer-binding transcription factor 4):** A transcription factor crucial for maintaining pluripotency in embryonic stem cells and expressed in seminomas.
    - **AFP (Alpha-fetoprotein):** Produced by the yolk sac, typically elevated in yolk sac tumors and embryonal carcinomas. Not produced by pure seminomas.
    - **β-hCG (Beta-human chorionic gonadotropin):** Produced by syncytiotrophoblasts, typically elevated in choriocarcinoma, but can also be elevated in embryonal carcinoma and sometimes in seminoma.
- **Clinical Significance:** Seminomas typically present as painless testicular masses. They are highly sensitive to radiation therapy and chemotherapy, making them generally curable even in advanced stages. The absence of AFP production is a key distinguishing feature from many other GCTs.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Yolk sac tumor:** This is the most common testicular tumor in infants but can occur in adults, often as part of a mixed GCT. Histologically, it shows Schiller-Duval bodies (glomerulus-like structures). It characteristically produces AFP. The patient's normal AFP and the described histology (uniform cells, clear cytoplasm, lymphocytic infiltrate) are inconsistent with yolk sac tumor.
- (B) **Embryonal carcinoma:** This is a malignant GCT that often presents as a hemorrhagic, necrotic mass. Histologically, it shows primitive, pleomorphic epithelial cells forming glands, rosettes, or papillary structures. It can produce AFP and/or β-hCG. The uniform cell population, clear cytoplasm, and lack of pleomorphism described in the vignette are inconsistent with embryonal carcinoma.
- (C) **Choriocarcinoma:** This is a highly malignant GCT composed of cytotrophoblasts and syncytiotrophoblasts. It typically presents with early hematogenous metastasis (lungs, brain) and often causes gynecomastia due to high β-hCG levels. Histologically, it shows biphasic proliferation of cytotrophoblasts and syncytiotrophoblasts. While β-hCG can be elevated in seminoma, the histological description and the typically very high β-hCG levels in choriocarcinoma are not consistent with this case. Also, choriocarcinoma does not typically produce AFP.
- (E) **Sertoli cell tumor:** This is a sex cord-stromal tumor, not a germ cell tumor. It arises from Sertoli cells. Histologically, it shows cells arranged in cords or tubules, often with eosinophilic cytoplasm and sometimes Reinke crystals. It does not produce AFP or β-hCG. The clinical presentation (young adult male) and the histological features (uniform large cells, clear cytoplasm, lymphocytic infiltrate, PLAP/OCT3/4 positivity) are inconsistent with a Sertoli cell tumor.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Seminoma                                  | Yolk Sac Tumor                     | Embryonal Carcinoma                 | Choriocarcinoma                      | Sertoli Cell Tumor                 |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------|------------------------------------|-------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------------|
| **Cell of Origin**   | Germ Cell                                 | Germ Cell                          | Germ Cell                           | Germ Cell                            | Sex Cord-Stromal (Sertoli Cell) |
| **Age**              | 15-35 years (peak)                        | Infants (common), Adults (mixed)    | 15-35 years                         | 20-40 years                          | Variable                           |
| **Presentation**     | Painless testicular mass                  | Painless testicular mass           | Painless testicular mass            | Often metastatic, gynecomastia       | Painless testicular mass           |
| **Gross Appearance** | Homogeneous, tan-white, lobulated         | Often hemorrhagic/necrotic         | Often hemorrhagic/necrotic          | Often hemorrhagic/necrotic           | Variable                           |
| **Histology**        | Uniform cells, clear cytoplasm, lymphocytes | Schiller-Duval bodies              | Pleomorphic cells, glands, rosettes | Biphasic (cyto/syncytioblasts)       | Cords/tubules, Reinke crystals     |
| **AFP**              | Negative                                  | Positive                            | Positive (often)                    | Negative                             | Negative                           |
| **β-hCG**            | Sometimes slightly elevated (10-15%)      | Negative                            | Positive (often)                    | Positive (usually very high)         | Negative                           |
| **PLAP**             | Positive                                  | Variable                            | Variable                            | Variable                             | Negative                           |
| **OCT3/4**           | Positive                                  | Variable                            | Variable                            | Variable                             | Negative                           |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Seminoma is the most common type of testicular germ cell tumor, characterized histologically by uniform cells with clear cytoplasm and a lymphocytic infiltrate, and immunophenotypically by positivity for PLAP and OCT3/4 and negativity for AFP. Differentiating seminoma from other GCTs (like embryonal carcinoma, yolk sac tumor, choriocarcinoma) relies on histological features, tumor marker levels (especially AFP), and immunohistochemistry. Seminoma is typically sensitive to radiation therapy. Key differentials include non-seminomatous GCTs, which often produce AFP and/or β-hCG and have different histological features. Sex cord-stromal tumors (like Sertoli cell tumors) are distinct entities with different origins and characteristics.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Male Genital Pathology
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis, histological features, and molecular mechanisms underlying testicular germ cell tumors (TGCTs), specifically seminoma, and its distinction from other testicular neoplasms.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of a painless, firm mass in his right testicle, which he noticed approximately 3 months ago. He denies any history of trauma, fever, or urinary symptoms. Physical examination reveals a palpable, non-tender, solid mass occupying the upper pole of the right testicle. The contralateral testicle is normal. Serum tumor markers, including alpha-fetoprotein (AFP), beta-human chorionic gonadotropin (β-hCG), and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), are all within normal limits. An inguinal radical orchiectomy is performed. Gross examination of the specimen shows a well-circumscribed, homogeneous, tan-white mass replacing the testicular parenchyma. Microscopic examination reveals sheets of relatively uniform, large cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and distinct cell borders, interspersed with a lymphocytic infiltrate. The tumor cells are positive for PLAP (placental alkaline phosphatase) and OCT3/4 immunohistochemistry, and negative for AFP and β-hCG. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Yolk sac tumor
(B) Embryonal carcinoma
(C) Choriocarcinoma
(D) Seminoma
(E) Sertoli cell tumor

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male (peak incidence age for TGCTs is 15-35 years).
*   **Presenting Symptom:** Painless, firm testicular mass (classic presentation for TGCTs).
*   **Duration:** 3 months (suggests a relatively slow-growing process).
*   **Physical Exam:** Palpable, non-tender, solid mass in the testicle.
*   **Tumor Markers:** AFP, β-hCG, and LDH are normal. This is a crucial clue. Seminomas are typically associated with normal AFP levels and may have elevated β-hCG in about 10-15% of cases, but elevation is usually not as high as in non-seminomatous germ cell tumors (NSGCTs). Normal AFP and β-hCG strongly point towards seminoma.
*   **Gross Pathology:** Well-circumscribed, homogeneous, tan-white mass (characteristic appearance of seminoma).
*   **Microscopic Pathology:** Sheets of large cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, distinct cell borders, and lymphocytic infiltrate (classic histological features of seminoma). The lymphocytic infiltrate is a key feature, often granulomatous.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Positive for PLAP and OCT3/4, negative for AFP and β-hCG (confirms seminoma origin and lack of differentiation towards yolk sac tumor or choriocarcinoma). PLAP and OCT3/4 are markers for germ cells, and seminomas typically express them. AFP and β-hCG are markers for yolk sac tumor and choriocarcinoma/syncytiotrophoblasts, respectively.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Testicular Germ Cell Tumors (TGCTs):** Arise from primordial germ cells within the seminiferous tubules. Classified into seminomas and non-seminomatous germ cell tumors (NSGCTs).
*   **Seminoma:** The most common type of TGCT (~50%). Typically occurs in the 3rd-4th decades. Characterized histologically by large, uniform cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, fibrous septa, and a prominent lymphocytic infiltrate (often granulomatous). Immunostaining is positive for PLAP, OCT3/4, KIT (CD117), and usually negative for AFP and β-hCG. Serum AFP is typically normal. β-hCG may be elevated in ~10-15% of cases.
*   **Non-Seminomatous Germ Cell Tumors (NSGCTs):** Include embryonal carcinoma, yolk sac tumor, choriocarcinoma, and teratoma. Often occur in younger patients (2nd-3rd decades). Frequently present with elevated serum AFP and/or β-hCG. Histologically heterogeneous.
    *   **Yolk Sac Tumor:** Produces AFP. Histology shows Schiller-Duval bodies (glomerulus-like structures).
    *   **Embryonal Carcinoma:** Aggressive, often mixed with other NSGCT components. Histology shows primitive, pleomorphic cells forming glands, rosettes, or papillary structures. Often produces AFP and/or β-hCG.
    *   **Choriocarcinoma:** Highly malignant, metastasizes early. Histology shows cytotrophoblasts and syncytiotrophoblasts. Produces high levels of β-hCG.
    *   **Teratoma:** Contains tissues derived from all three germ layers (ectoderm, mesoderm, endoderm). Can be mature or immature. Usually does not produce AFP or β-hCG unless containing yolk sac tumor or choriocarcinoma elements.
*   **Sertoli Cell Tumor:** Arises from Sertoli cells, not germ cells. Usually benign. Can produce estrogen, leading to feminization in males. Histology shows cells arranged in cords or tubules. Not associated with germ cell markers like PLAP or OCT3/4, nor AFP or β-hCG.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Yolk sac tumor:** Incorrect. Yolk sac tumors are a type of NSGCT. They typically produce AFP, which was normal in this patient. Histologically, they are characterized by Schiller-Duval bodies, not the uniform cells with clear cytoplasm and lymphocytic infiltrate seen here. Immunohistochemistry would be positive for AFP.
- (B) **Embryonal carcinoma:** Incorrect. Embryonal carcinoma is another type of NSGCT. It is often associated with elevated AFP and/or β-hCG. Histologically, it shows primitive, pleomorphic cells, unlike the uniform cells described. Immunohistochemistry would be positive for PLAP and OCT3/4 but often also positive for AFP and/or β-hCG.
- (C) **Choriocarcinoma:** Incorrect. Choriocarcinoma is a highly malignant NSGCT characterized by cytotrophoblasts and syncytiotrophoblasts. It produces very high levels of β-hCG, which was normal in this patient. Histologically distinct from the description provided.
- (E) **Sertoli cell tumor:** Incorrect. Sertoli cell tumors arise from stromal cells (Sertoli cells), not germ cells. They do not produce AFP or β-hCG. Histologically, they show cells arranged in cords or tubules, distinct from the sheets of large cells with clear cytoplasm seen in seminoma. They are negative for germ cell markers like PLAP and OCT3/4.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature             | Seminoma                                  | Yolk Sac Tumor                       | Embryonal Carcinoma                  | Choriocarcinoma                     | Sertoli Cell Tumor                 |
| :------------------ | :---------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- |
| **Cell Origin**     | Germ Cell                                 | Germ Cell                            | Germ Cell                            | Germ Cell                           | Stromal Cell (Sertoli Cell)       |
| **Age**             | 30s-40s                                   | 20s-30s                              | 20s-30s                              | 20s-30s                             | Any age                            |
| **Tumor Markers**   | AFP (-), β-hCG (± low)                    | AFP (+)                              | AFP (+/-), β-hCG (+/-)               | β-hCG (++)                          | AFP (-), β-hCG (-)                 |
| **Histology**       | Uniform cells, clear cytoplasm, granulomas | Schiller-Duval bodies                | Primitive, pleomorphic cells         | Cytotropho/Syncytiotrophoblasts     | Cords/tubules of Sertoli cells     |
| **Immunohistology** | PLAP (+), OCT3/4 (+), AFP (-), β-hCG (-) | AFP (+), PLAP (+/-), OCT3/4 (+/-)     | PLAP (+), OCT3/4 (+), AFP (+/-), β-hCG (+/-) | β-hCG (+), PLAP (+/-), OCT3/4 (+/-) | PLAP (-), OCT3/4 (-), AFP (-), β-hCG (-) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Seminomas are the most common type of testicular germ cell tumor, typically presenting as a painless mass in young adults. Normal serum AFP and characteristic histology (uniform cells, clear cytoplasm, lymphocytic infiltrate) combined with positive PLAP/OCT3/4 and negative AFP/β-hCG immunohistochemistry are diagnostic. Differentiating seminoma from NSGCTs (yolk sac tumor, embryonal carcinoma, choriocarcinoma) is crucial, as NSGCTs often produce AFP and/or β-hCG and require different management strategies. Sertoli cell tumors are stromal tumors, not germ cell tumors, and have distinct histological and immunohistochemical features.

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Male Genital Pathology: Focus 03-45 (Male Pathology)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis, histological features, and molecular markers of prostate cancer, specifically distinguishing it from benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH) and other prostatic conditions, and recognizing the significance of Gleason score. (理解前列腺癌的病理发生、组织学特征和分子标志物，特别是将其与良性前列腺增生 (BPH) 和其他前列腺状况区分开来，并认识到格里森评分的重要性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male presents to his primary care physician for a routine check-up. He reports increased urinary frequency, nocturia, and a weak urinary stream over the past year. He denies hematuria, dysuria, or flank pain. His past medical history is significant for hypertension and hyperlipidemia, both well-controlled with medication. Digital rectal examination (DRE) reveals a firm, nodular prostate gland, approximately 4 cm in diameter, with asymmetry and induration in the right lobe. Serum prostate-specific antigen (PSA) level is elevated at 18 ng/mL (normal < 4 ng/mL). Transrectal ultrasound (TRUS)-guided biopsy of the prostate yields 12 core samples. Histopathological examination of the biopsy specimens reveals glands with varying architectural patterns, ranging from well-formed glands resembling normal prostate tissue to poorly formed glands with fused cribriform patterns and solid sheets of tumor cells. The tumor cells exhibit enlarged nuclei, prominent nucleoli, and loss of normal glandular polarity. Immunohistochemical staining shows positivity for prostate-specific acid phosphatase (PSAP) and prostate-specific membrane antigen (PSMA). The Gleason score is calculated based on the predominant patterns observed in the biopsy cores. Which of the following histological features is MOST characteristic of the malignant process described in this patient?

(A) Stromal hyperplasia with smooth muscle proliferation and lymphocytic infiltration.
(B) Glandular atrophy with extensive calcification and squamous metaplasia.
(C) Infiltrative growth pattern with loss of basal cell layer and nuclear atypia.
(D) Cystic dilation of glands with hemosiderin deposition and inflammatory cell aggregates.
(E) Diffuse fibrosis with acinar destruction and formation of corpora amylacea.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male, age group common for prostate cancer and BPH.
- **Symptoms:** Lower urinary tract symptoms (LUTS) - frequency, nocturia, weak stream. These are common in both BPH and prostate cancer.
- **DRE Findings:** Firm, nodular, asymmetric, indurated prostate. These findings are suspicious for malignancy, although BPH can also cause enlargement. Induration is a key sign suggesting cancer.
- **PSA Level:** Elevated at 18 ng/mL. While elevated PSA can be seen in BPH, prostatitis, and urinary retention, levels >10 ng/mL are highly suggestive of prostate cancer.
- **Biopsy Findings:** Histopathology describes glands with varying patterns (well-formed to poorly formed, cribriform, solid sheets), enlarged nuclei, prominent nucleoli, loss of normal glandular polarity. These are classic features of adenocarcinoma, the most common type of prostate cancer.
- **Immunohistochemistry:** PSAP and PSMA positivity confirm prostatic origin.
- **Gleason Score:** Mentioned, indicating the grading system used for prostate cancer based on architectural patterns.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST characteristic histological feature of the *malignant process* (prostate cancer).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Prostate Cancer Pathogenesis:** Prostate cancer arises from the glandular epithelium of the prostate. It is characterized by uncontrolled proliferation of these cells, leading to the formation of malignant glands.
- **Histological Features of Prostate Cancer:** Key features include:
    - **Architectural Patterns:** Variable, ranging from well-differentiated (Gleason pattern 3) to poorly differentiated (Gleason pattern 5). Includes acinar, papillary, cribriform, solid, and comedo patterns.
    - **Cytological Features:** Nuclear atypia (enlarged, hyperchromatic nuclei), prominent nucleoli, loss of normal glandular polarity, increased nuclear-to-cytoplasmic ratio.
    - **Growth Pattern:** Infiltrative growth into the surrounding stroma, often disrupting the normal architecture.
    - **Basal Cell Layer:** Loss or disruption of the normal basal cell layer lining the glands is a hallmark of malignancy. Normal prostate glands have a distinct basal cell layer.
- **Gleason Score:** A grading system based on the sum of the two most common architectural patterns (Gleason patterns 3-5). Higher scores indicate more aggressive tumors.
- **Distinguishing from BPH:** BPH involves stromal and epithelial hyperplasia, smooth muscle proliferation, lymphocytic infiltration, and cystic changes, but *not* the nuclear atypia, loss of basal cell layer, or infiltrative growth seen in cancer.
- **Molecular Markers:** PSAP and PSMA are expressed by both normal and malignant prostatic epithelium, but their expression patterns and intensity can differ. TMPRSS2-ERG fusion is a common genetic alteration in prostate cancer.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Stromal hyperplasia with smooth muscle proliferation and lymphocytic infiltration:** This describes features characteristic of Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia (BPH), not prostate cancer. BPH involves an increase in both stromal and epithelial components, often with inflammation.
- (B) **Glandular atrophy with extensive calcification and squamous metaplasia:** Glandular atrophy and calcification can occur in the prostate, sometimes associated with chronic inflammation or aging, but are not primary features of prostate cancer. Squamous metaplasia is uncommon in the prostate and usually associated with chronic inflammation or irritation, not malignancy itself.
- (C) **Infiltrative growth pattern with loss of basal cell layer and nuclear atypia:** This accurately describes the key histological hallmarks of prostate adenocarcinoma. The loss of the basal cell layer is a critical feature distinguishing malignant glands from benign ones. Infiltrative growth and nuclear atypia (enlarged nuclei, prominent nucleoli) are also characteristic of malignancy. This aligns perfectly with the biopsy description in the vignette.
- (D) **Cystic dilation of glands with hemosiderin deposition and inflammatory cell aggregates:** Cystic dilation can be seen in BPH or prostatitis. Hemosiderin deposition suggests prior hemorrhage or inflammation. Inflammatory cell aggregates are common in prostatitis or BPH. These features are not specific to or characteristic of prostate cancer itself.
- (E) **Diffuse fibrosis with acinar destruction and formation of corpora amylacea:** Diffuse fibrosis can occur in chronic prostatitis or after treatment. Acinar destruction is non-specific. Corpora amylacea are laminated concretions found in the prostatic stroma, common in aging and BPH, but not characteristic of cancer.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature                     | Prostate Cancer (Adenocarcinoma) | Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia (BPH) | Prostatitis (Chronic) |
| :-------------------------- | :-------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :------------------- |
| **Glandular Architecture**   | Variable (well-formed to poorly formed, cribriform, solid) | Well-formed, regular glands        | Variable, may show inflammation |
| **Basal Cell Layer**        | Lost or disrupted                 | Present, intact                    | Present, intact      |
| **Nuclear Atypia**           | Present (enlarged, hyperchromatic nuclei, prominent nucleoli) | Absent                             | Absent               |
| **Growth Pattern**           | Infiltrative                      | Expansive, nodular                  | Variable             |
| **Stromal Changes**         | Fibrosis, inflammation            | Hyperplasia, smooth muscle proliferation, lymphocytic infiltration | Inflammation, fibrosis |
| **Other Features**          | PSMA+, PSAP+                      | PSMA+, PSAP+                      | Variable             |
| **Common Symptoms**         | LUTS, hematuria (less common)     | LUTS                               | LUTS, pain, dysuria  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prostate cancer is characterized by malignant glands exhibiting nuclear atypia, loss of the basal cell layer, and infiltrative growth. These features distinguish it from BPH, which shows stromal and epithelial hyperplasia with intact basal cells and no significant nuclear atypia. Recognizing these histological hallmarks is crucial for diagnosis and grading (Gleason score). Key differentials include BPH and prostatitis, which can present with similar LUTS but have distinct histological features.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Male Genital Pathology: Focus 03-46 (LEARNINGOBJECTIVES)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis, histological features, and molecular markers of Sertoli cell tumors, specifically Sertoli cell adenoma and Sertoli cell carcinoma, and differentiating them from other testicular neoplasms. (理解精索细胞瘤的病理发生、组织学特征和分子标记，以及与其他睾丸肿瘤的鉴别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of a painless, slowly enlarging mass in his right testicle, noticed over the past 6 months. He denies any history of trauma, fever, or urinary symptoms. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender mass approximately 5 cm in diameter within the right testicle. The left testicle is normal. Serum tumor markers, including alpha-fetoprotein (AFP), beta-human chorionic gonadotropin (β-hCG), and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), are all within normal limits. A radical inguinal orchiectomy is performed. Gross examination of the specimen reveals a well-circumscribed, solid, yellowish-tan tumor replacing the testicular parenchyma. Microscopic examination shows nests and cords of large, polygonal cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and indistinct cell borders, arranged in a tubular pattern. Scattered Leydig cells are also present within the tumor. Immunohistochemical staining reveals the tumor cells are positive for inhibin, calretinin, and SF-1 (Steroidogenic Factor 1), but negative for PLAP (Placental Alkaline Phosphatase), AFP, β-hCG, and CD117 (c-KIT). The patient is asymptomatic post-operatively.

Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Seminoma
(B) Yolk Sac Tumor
(C) Sertoli Cell Tumor
(D) Leydig Cell Tumor
(E) Granulosa Cell Tumor

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male. This age is less common for typical germ cell tumors (which peak in younger men) but can occur for sex cord-stromal tumors.
*   **Presentation:** Painless, slowly enlarging testicular mass (5 cm) for 6 months. This presentation is common for many testicular tumors, both germ cell and sex cord-stromal.
*   **Normal Tumor Markers:** AFP, β-hCG, and LDH are normal. This is a crucial clue. Normal markers make typical germ cell tumors like seminoma (can elevate β-hCG, LDH), yolk sac tumor (elevates AFP), embryonal carcinoma (elevates AFP, β-hCG, LDH), and choriocarcinoma (elevates β-hCG) less likely.
*   **Gross Pathology:** Well-circumscribed, solid, yellowish-tan tumor. This description is non-specific but consistent with several testicular tumors.
*   **Microscopic Pathology:** Nests and cords of large, polygonal cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, indistinct cell borders, arranged in a tubular pattern. Scattered Leydig cells are present. This morphology is characteristic of Sertoli cells. The presence of scattered Leydig cells is common in Sertoli cell tumors.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Positive for inhibin, calretinin, and SF-1; negative for PLAP, AFP, β-hCG, and CD117. This is the definitive diagnostic information.
    *   Inhibin and calretinin are markers for sex cord-stromal tumors, including Sertoli and granulosa cells.
    *   SF-1 is a nuclear transcription factor expressed in both Sertoli and granulosa cells.
    *   PLAP is typically positive in seminoma and yolk sac tumor.
    *   AFP and β-hCG are germ cell markers.
    *   CD117 (c-KIT) is often positive in Leydig cell tumors and gastrointestinal stromal tumors (GISTs).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Sertoli Cell Tumors:** These are rare sex cord-stromal tumors arising from Sertoli cells, which normally support spermatogenesis. They can occur at any age but are most common in children and young adults, with a second peak in middle age. They can be benign (adenoma) or malignant (carcinoma).
*   **Pathogenesis:** The exact cause is unknown, but genetic alterations (e.g., mutations in *DAXX* or *WT1*) have been implicated.
*   **Histology:** Characterized by nests and cords of large cells with eosinophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and indistinct cell borders, often arranged in tubules. Scattered Leydig cells are frequently present.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Key markers are inhibin, calretinin, and SF-1. These markers help distinguish Sertoli cell tumors from germ cell tumors (negative for inhibin/calretinin) and Leydig cell tumors (typically negative for inhibin/calretinin, positive for CD117/calretinin).
*   **Clinical Features:** Often present as a painless testicular mass. Tumor markers are typically normal. Malignant potential depends on histological features (e.g., vascular invasion, necrosis, high mitotic rate).
*   **Differential Diagnosis:** The key is distinguishing from other testicular tumors based on histology and immunohistochemistry. Seminoma (PLAP+, often β-hCG+) and yolk sac tumor (AFP+, PLAP+) are germ cell tumors. Leydig cell tumors (CD117+, inhibin variable) and granulosa cell tumors (inhibin+, calretinin+, SF-1+, often estrogen production) are other sex cord-stromal tumors.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Seminoma:** Seminoma is the most common type of testicular germ cell tumor. Histologically, it shows sheets of uniform, large cells with clear cytoplasm and prominent nucleoli, often with lymphocytic infiltrate. It typically stains positive for PLAP and may stain positive for β-hCG or OCT3/4. The tumor markers (AFP, β-hCG, LDH) can be elevated, although AFP is typically normal. The immunohistochemistry in the vignette (inhibin+, calretinin+, SF-1+, PLAP-) rules out seminoma.
*   **(B) Yolk Sac Tumor:** This is a germ cell tumor, often occurring in infants and young children, but can occur in adults. Histologically, it shows various patterns, including microcystic, reticular, solid, and Schiller-Duval bodies (pathognomonic). It is strongly positive for AFP and PLAP. The normal AFP and negative PLAP in the vignette rule out yolk sac tumor.
*   **(D) Leydig Cell Tumor:** This is a sex cord-stromal tumor arising from Leydig cells, which produce testosterone. Histologically, it shows polygonal or round cells with abundant eosinophilic or clear cytoplasm (due to lipid droplets) and centrally located nuclei. They are typically positive for CD117 (c-KIT) and calretinin, and may stain positive for inhibin. While calretinin can be positive, the strong positivity for inhibin, calretinin, and SF-1, along with the characteristic morphology (large polygonal cells with indistinct borders, tubular arrangement) and negativity for CD117, makes Sertoli cell tumor more likely. Leydig cell tumors are also more likely to cause endocrine symptoms (gynecomastia, precocious puberty).
*   **(E) Granulosa Cell Tumor:** This is another sex cord-stromal tumor, arising from granulosa cells. Histologically, it shows cells arranged in various patterns (microfollicular, macrofollicular, trabecular, diffuse). The cells have small, round nuclei and scant cytoplasm. They are positive for inhibin, calretinin, and SF-1, similar to Sertoli cell tumors. However, the morphology described in the vignette (large polygonal cells with abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, indistinct borders, tubular pattern) is much more characteristic of Sertoli cell tumor than granulosa cell tumor. Granulosa cell tumors are also more likely to produce estrogen, leading to endocrine symptoms.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Sertoli Cell Tumor | Seminoma          | Yolk Sac Tumor    | Leydig Cell Tumor | Granulosa Cell Tumor |
| :------------------ | :----------------- | :---------------- | :--------------- | :---------------- | :------------------- |
| **Origin**          | Sertoli Cell       | Germ Cell         | Germ Cell        | Leydig Cell       | Granulosa Cell       |
| **Age**             | Any (peaks young/mid) | Young/Middle Adult | Infant/Young Child | Any               | Middle/Older Adult   |
| **Tumor Markers**   | Normal             | β-hCG, LDH +/-    | AFP              | Normal            | Normal               |
| **Histology**       | Large polygonal cells, eosinophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, indistinct borders, tubular pattern | Uniform large cells, clear cytoplasm, lymphocytic infiltrate | Variable (microcystic, Schiller-Duval bodies) | Polygonal/round cells, eosinophilic/clear cytoplasm, lipid droplets | Small cells, round nuclei, scant cytoplasm, microfollicular pattern |
| **Immunohistochemistry** | Inhibin+, Calretinin+, SF-1+, PLAP-, AFP-, β-hCG-, CD117- | PLAP+, OCT3/4+, β-hCG+/- | AFP+, PLAP+        | CD117+, Calretinin+, Inhibin+/- | Inhibin+, Calretinin+, SF-1+, CD117- |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Sertoli cell tumors are rare sex cord-stromal tumors characterized by specific immunohistochemical markers (inhibin+, calretinin+, SF-1+) and distinct morphology. Differentiating them from germ cell tumors (normal markers, PLAP-) and other sex cord-stromal tumors (Leydig cell tumor: CD117+, Granulosa cell tumor: morphology) relies heavily on immunohistochemistry and histological features. Normal tumor markers are a key feature distinguishing sex cord-stromal tumors from most germ cell tumors.

### Question 147: Male Genital Pathology: Focus 03-47 (❏Explain informationrelated to the pathology of th)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis, histological features, and molecular markers of Sertoli cell tumors, specifically Sertoli cell adenoma and its potential for malignancy, and differentiating it from other testicular neoplasms. (理解精索细胞瘤的发病机制、组织学特征和分子标记物，特别是精索细胞腺瘤及其恶性潜力，并将其与其他睾丸肿瘤区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of a painless, slowly enlarging mass in his right testicle, noticed over the past 6 months. He denies any history of trauma, fever, or urinary symptoms. Physical examination reveals a firm, non-tender, 5 cm mass confined to the right testicle, without palpable lymphadenopathy. Serum tumor markers, including alpha-fetoprotein (AFP), beta-human chorionic gonadotropin (β-hCG), and lactate dehydrogenase (LDH), are all within normal limits. A radical inguinal orchiectomy is performed. Gross examination of the specimen shows a well-circumscribed, solid, yellowish-tan tumor replacing the testicular parenchyma. Microscopically, the tumor is composed of large, polygonal cells arranged in cords and nests, separated by delicate fibrovascular stroma. The tumor cells have abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, and round to oval nuclei with finely dispersed chromatin. Immunohistochemical staining reveals strong positivity for inhibin, calretinin, and D2-40, and negativity for AFP, β-hCG, PLAP, and CD117. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

(A) Seminoma
(B) Embryonal Carcinoma
(C) Sertoli Cell Tumor
(D) Leydig Cell Tumor
(E) Granulosa Cell Tumor

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male. Testicular tumors are most common in young men (15-35 years), but Sertoli cell tumors can occur at any age, including older adults.
*   **Presentation:** Painless, slowly enlarging testicular mass (5 cm). This is a classic presentation for testicular cancer, although the slow growth over 6 months is somewhat less typical for aggressive germ cell tumors.
*   **Physical Exam:** Firm, non-tender mass confined to the testicle. No lymphadenopathy. Consistent with a primary testicular tumor.
*   **Tumor Markers:** AFP, β-hCG, LDH are normal. This is crucial. Normal markers strongly argue against non-seminomatous germ cell tumors (NSGCTs) like embryonal carcinoma or yolk sac tumor (which often elevates AFP) and choriocarcinoma (which elevates β-hCG). Seminomas can sometimes elevate β-hCG but typically do not elevate AFP or LDH significantly. Normal markers point towards a sex cord-stromal tumor or a seminoma.
*   **Gross Pathology:** Well-circumscribed, solid, yellowish-tan tumor. Non-specific but consistent with a neoplasm.
*   **Microscopic Pathology:** Large, polygonal cells arranged in cords and nests, separated by delicate fibrovascular stroma. Abundant eosinophilic cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, round nuclei with finely dispersed chromatin. This description is highly characteristic of Sertoli cells.
*   **Immunohistochemistry (IHC):** Strong positivity for inhibin, calretinin, and D2-40. Negativity for AFP, β-hCG, PLAP, and CD117. This IHC profile is the definitive clue.
    *   Inhibin and calretinin are markers for sex cord-stromal tumors, particularly Sertoli and granulosa cells.
    *   D2-40 is a marker for lymphatic endothelium, often found in the stroma of Sertoli cell tumors.
    *   Negativity for AFP, β-hCG, PLAP (a marker for seminoma and some NSGCTs), and CD117 (c-KIT, often positive in GISTs and some testicular tumors) helps rule out germ cell tumors and other stromal tumors.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Sertoli Cell Tumors:** These are rare sex cord-stromal tumors arising from Sertoli cells, which normally support spermatogenesis. They can occur at any age but are more common in children and young adults, with a second peak in middle age. They can be benign (adenoma) or malignant (sarcoma).
*   **Pathogenesis:** The exact cause is unknown, but genetic factors may play a role. Some are associated with Peutz-Jeghers syndrome.
*   **Histology:** Composed of cells resembling Sertoli cells, often arranged in cords or nests separated by fibrous stroma. Nuclear features can vary, but often include prominent nucleoli and eosinophilic cytoplasm.
*   **Immunohistochemistry:** Key diagnostic feature. Positive for inhibin, calretinin, D2-40, and sometimes estrogen receptor (ER) and progesterone receptor (PR). Negative for germ cell markers (AFP, β-hCG, PLAP) and Leydig cell markers (calpain, INSM1).
*   **Clinical Features:** Often present as a painless testicular mass. May produce estrogen, leading to gynecomastia or feminization in males. Tumor markers are typically normal.
*   **Malignancy:** Malignant potential is difficult to predict based on histology alone. Features like vascular invasion, necrosis, high mitotic rate, and large size (>5 cm) are associated with increased risk. IHC markers like Ki-67 proliferation index can also be helpful.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Seminoma:** This is the most common type of testicular germ cell tumor. It typically occurs in young adults. While it can present as a painless mass and tumor markers may be normal or only slightly elevated (β-hCG), the histology (uniform cells with clear cytoplasm, prominent nucleoli, fibrous septa with lymphocytes) and IHC (positive for PLAP, OCT3/4, CD117; negative for inhibin, calretinin) are distinct from the case description. The described histology (polygonal cells, eosinophilic cytoplasm, cords/nests) and IHC (positive inhibin, calretinin, D2-40) are inconsistent with seminoma.
*   **(B) Embryonal Carcinoma:** A type of non-seminomatous germ cell tumor (NSGCT). It is aggressive and often presents with elevated AFP and/or β-hCG. Histology shows primitive, pleomorphic cells forming sheets, cords, or tubules. IHC is typically negative for sex cord-stromal markers like inhibin and calretinin and positive for germ cell markers like PLAP, OCT3/4, and sometimes CD117. The normal tumor markers and the specific histology/IHC profile rule this out.
*   **(D) Leydig Cell Tumor:** Another sex cord-stromal tumor, arising from Leydig cells. These cells produce testosterone. They can occur at any age. Histology shows polygonal or round cells with abundant granular eosinophilic cytoplasm (due to Reinke crystals) and round nuclei. They are typically positive for calpain, INSM1, and sometimes inhibin, but negative for calretinin and D2-40. They can cause precocious puberty in boys or gynecomastia/decreased libido in adults due to hormonal effects. The described histology (prominent nucleoli, cords/nests) and the specific IHC profile (strong calretinin, D2-40 positive, INSM1 negative) are inconsistent with a Leydig cell tumor.
*   **(E) Granulosa Cell Tumor:** A rare sex cord-stromal tumor, often occurring in older adults. It can produce estrogen, leading to feminization. Histology shows cells arranged in microfollicular patterns or sheets. IHC is positive for inhibin, calretinin, ER, PR, and FOXL2. While it shares positivity for inhibin and calretinin with Sertoli cell tumors, the characteristic microfollicular pattern is absent in the description, and D2-40 positivity is more typical for Sertoli cell tumors. FOXL2 positivity is a key marker for granulosa cell tumors, which was not mentioned as positive in the vignette. The overall histological description (cords/nests, polygonal cells, prominent nucleoli) fits Sertoli cell tumor better.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):
| Feature             | Sertoli Cell Tumor | Seminoma          | Embryonal Carcinoma | Leydig Cell Tumor | Granulosa Cell Tumor |
| :------------------ | :----------------- | :---------------- | :------------------ | :---------------- | :------------------- |
| **Age**             | Any                | Young Adult       | Young Adult         | Any               | Older Adult          |
| **Tumor Markers**   | Normal             | Normal/↑β-hCG     | ↑AFP &/or β-hCG     | Normal            | Normal               |
| **Histology**       | Cords/Nests, Polygonal cells, Eosinophilic cytoplasm, Prominent Nucleoli | Uniform cells, Clear cytoplasm, Lymphocytes | Primitive, Pleomorphic cells | Polygonal cells, Granular cytoplasm, Reinke crystals | Microfollicular pattern |
| **IHC**             | +Inhibin, +Calretinin, +D2-40, -AFP, -β-hCG, -PLAP, -CD117 | +PLAP, +OCT3/4, +CD117, -Inhibin, -Calretinin | +PLAP, +OCT3/4, -Inhibin, -Calretinin | +Calpain, +INSM1, +/-Inhibin, -Calretinin, -D2-40 | +Inhibin, +Calretinin, +ER, +PR, +FOXL2, -D2-40 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Sertoli cell tumors are rare sex cord-stromal tumors of the testis characterized by specific histological features (cords/nests of polygonal cells) and a distinctive immunohistochemical profile (positive for inhibin, calretinin, D2-40; negative for germ cell markers). Normal tumor markers and this specific IHC profile are key to differentiating them from germ cell tumors (seminoma, embryonal carcinoma) and other stromal tumors (Leydig cell tumor, granulosa cell tumor). Remember that D2-40 positivity is particularly suggestive of Sertoli cell tumors.
